=== BUGGY CODE ===
class Solution:
    def stoneGameVI(self, a: List[int], b: List[int]) -> int:
        # hurt other enemy rather than self collecting to max
        combines = [(a[i] + b[i], a[i], b[i]) for i in range(len(a))]
        combines.sort(reverse=True) # why we can sort? -> because we can pick any stone we want
        bobPoints = sum(b)
        alicePoints = 0
        for i in range(1, len(a), 2):
            alicePoints += combines[i][1]
            bobPoints -= combines[i][2]
        if alicePoints > bobPoints:
            return 1
        elif alicePoints < bobPoints:
            return -1
        return 0

```python
class Solution:
    def stoneGameVI(self, a: List[int], b: List[int]) -> int:
        # hurt other enemy rather than self collecting to max
        combines = [(a[i] + b[i], a[i], b[i]) for i in range(len(a))]
        combines.sort(reverse=True) # why we can sort? -> because we can pick any stone we want
        bobPoints = sum(b)
        alicePoints = 0
        for i in range(1, len(a), 2):
            alicePoints += combines[i][1]
            bobPoints -= combines[i][2]
        if alicePoints > bobPoints:
            return 1
        elif alicePoints < bobPoints:
            return -1
        return 0
```

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def stoneGameVI(self, a: List[int], b: List[int]) -> int:
        combines = [(a[i] + b[i], a[i], b[i]) for i in range(len(a))]
        combines.sort(reverse=True)

        alicePoints = 0
        bobPoints = sum(b)

        for i in range(0, len(a), 2):
            alicePoints += combines[i][1]
            bobPoints -= combines[i][2]

        if alicePoints > bobPoints:
            return 1
        elif alicePoints < bobPoints:
            return -1
        return 0

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def stoneGameVI(self, a: List[int], b: List[int]) -> int:
        combines = [(a[i] + b[i], a[i], b[i]) for i in range(len(a))]
        combines.sort(reverse=True)

        alicePoints = 0
        bobPoints = sum(b)

        for i in range(0, len(a), 2):
            alicePoints += combines[i][1]
            bobPoints -= combines[i][2]

        if alicePoints > bobPoints:
            return 1
        elif alicePoints < bobPoints:
            return -1
        return 0

Let's think of "Stone Game VI" known solution:
```
def stoneGameVI(self, aliceValues, bobValues):
    values = [[a+b, a, b] for a,b in zip(aliceValues, bobValues)]
    values.sort(reverse=True)
    alice = sum(a for _,a,_ in values[::2])
    bob = sum(b for _,_,b in values[1::2])
    return (alice > bob) - (alice < bob)
```
This is common. Our code with `bobPoints = sum(b)` subtract is equivalent. Good.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

Need maybe if "faulty python code" has "one or more bugs" maybe there is a bug in "combines.sort(reverse=True)" because if we sort by a+b descending, but if Alice and Bob both optimal, the greedy choice is not simply sort by sum? Let's verify with a known counterexample? Wait, is Stone Game VI greedy by sum always optimal? Let's recall LeetCode 1686. The solution is indeed sort by `a[i]+b[i]` descending, then alternate. But let's prove because there is a subtlety: Each stone has value to Alice and Bob. If Alice takes stone i, her net advantage over Bob is a_i + b_i? Let's formalize: Suppose current player chooses stone i. The difference in final scores (current player's score - other player's score) changes by a_i + b_i relative to if other player chose it? Actually if Alice takes i, she gains a_i and Bob doesn't gain b_i, so swing = a_i + b_i. If Bob takes i, swing in Bob's favor = a_i + b_i. Thus each stone contributes its sum to whoever takes it in terms of score difference. Since the total number of stones each player takes is fixed (Alice gets ceil(n/2), Bob floor(n/2)), the optimal is to assign stones with largest sums to Alice? Wait, if each stone's "value" to the taker is a_i+b_i in terms of score difference, then both players want the largest sums. Alice picks largest, Bob next, etc. This is like both players value each stone equally (a_i+b_i), so greedy by sum is optimal. Yes.

But let's double-check with a potential counterexample: a=[8,7,1], b=[0,0,100]? Sums: 8,7,101. Greedy: Alice picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=8,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=7,b=0). Alice=8, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice instead picks stone1 (a=8), Bob picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=7). Alice=15, Bob=100, Bob wins. Greedy good. Another: a=[5,4,100], b=[100,4,0], sums 105,8,100. Greedy Alice picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Bob picks stone3 (a=100,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=4,b=4). Alice=9, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice picks stone3 (a=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=4). Alice=104, Bob=100, Alice wins too. Greedy okay. Need no counterexample.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. If in some strategy Alice gets j and Bob gets i, swapping them (Alice gets i, Bob gets j) changes Alice score by a_i - a_j and Bob score by b_j - b_i? Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

Need maybe if "faulty python code" has "one or more bugs" maybe there is a bug in "combines.sort(reverse=True)" because if we sort by a+b descending, but if Alice and Bob both optimal, the greedy choice is not simply sort by sum? Let's verify with a known counterexample? Wait, is Stone Game VI greedy by sum always optimal? Let's recall LeetCode 1686. The solution is indeed sort by `a[i]+b[i]` descending, then alternate. But let's prove because there is a subtlety: Each stone has value to Alice and Bob. If Alice takes stone i, her net advantage over Bob is a_i + b_i? Let's formalize: Suppose current player chooses stone i. The difference in final scores (current player's score - other player's score) changes by a_i + b_i relative to if other player chose it? Actually if Alice takes i, she gains a_i and Bob doesn't gain b_i, so swing = a_i + b_i. If Bob takes i, swing in Bob's favor = a_i + b_i. Thus each stone contributes its sum to whoever takes it in terms of score difference. Since the total number of stones each player takes is fixed (Alice gets ceil(n/2), Bob floor(n/2)), the optimal is to assign stones with largest sums to Alice? Wait, if each stone's "value" to the taker is a_i+b_i in terms of score difference, then both players want the largest sums. Alice picks largest, Bob next, etc. This is like both players value each stone equally (a_i+b_i), so greedy by sum is optimal. Yes.

But let's double-check with a potential counterexample: a=[8,7,1], b=[0,0,100]? Sums: 8,7,101. Greedy: Alice picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=8,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=7,b=0). Alice=8, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice instead picks stone1 (a=8), Bob picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=7). Alice=15, Bob=100, Bob wins. Greedy good. Another: a=[5,4,100], b=[100,4,0], sums 105,8,100. Greedy Alice picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Bob picks stone3 (a=100,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=4,b=4). Alice=9, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice picks stone3 (a=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=4). Alice=104, Bob=100, Alice wins too. Greedy okay. Need no counterexample.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. If in some strategy Alice gets j and Bob gets i, swapping them (Alice gets i, Bob gets j) changes Alice score by a_i - a_j and Bob score by b_j - b_i? Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

Let's verify with a known counterexample? Wait, is Stone Game VI greedy by sum always optimal? Let's recall LeetCode 1686. The solution is indeed sort by `a[i]+b[i]` descending, then alternate. But let's prove because there is a subtlety: Each stone has value to Alice and Bob. If Alice takes stone i, her net advantage over Bob is a_i + b_i? Let's formalize: Suppose current player chooses stone i. The difference in final scores (current player's score - other player's score) changes by a_i + b_i relative to if other player chose it? Actually if Alice takes i, she gains a_i and Bob doesn't gain b_i, so swing = a_i + b_i. If Bob takes i, swing in Bob's favor = a_i + b_i. Thus each stone contributes its sum to whoever takes it in terms of score difference. Since the total number of stones each player takes is fixed (Alice gets ceil(n/2), Bob floor(n/2)), the optimal is to assign stones with largest sums to Alice? Wait, if each stone's "value" to the taker is a_i+b_i in terms of score difference, then both players want the largest sums. Alice picks largest, Bob next, etc. This is like both players value each stone equally (a_i+b_i), so greedy by sum is optimal. Yes.

But let's double-check with a potential counterexample: a=[8,7,1], b=[0,0,100]? Sums: 8,7,101. Greedy: Alice picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=8,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=7,b=0). Alice=8, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice instead picks stone1 (a=8), Bob picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=7). Alice=15, Bob=100, Bob wins. Greedy good. Another: a=[5,4,100], b=[100,4,0], sums 105,8,100. Greedy Alice picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Bob picks stone3 (a=100,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=4,b=4). Alice=9, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice picks stone3 (a=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=4). Alice=104, Bob=100, Alice wins too. Greedy okay. Need no counterexample.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. If in some strategy Alice gets j and Bob gets i, swapping them (Alice gets i, Bob gets j) changes Alice score by a_i - a_j and Bob score by b_j - b_i? Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

Let's verify with a known counterexample? Wait, is Stone Game VI greedy by sum always optimal? Let's recall LeetCode 1686. The solution is indeed sort by `a[i]+b[i]` descending, then alternate. But let's prove because there is a subtlety: Each stone has value to Alice and Bob. If Alice takes stone i, her net advantage over Bob is a_i + b_i? Let's formalize: Suppose current player chooses stone i. The difference in final scores (current player's score - other player's score) changes by a_i + b_i relative to if other player chose it? Actually if Alice takes i, she gains a_i and Bob doesn't gain b_i, so swing = a_i + b_i. If Bob takes i, swing in Bob's favor = a_i + b_i. Thus each stone contributes its sum to whoever takes it in terms of score difference. Since the total number of stones each player takes is fixed (Alice gets ceil(n/2), Bob floor(n/2)), the optimal is to assign stones with largest sums to Alice? Wait, if each stone's "value" to the taker is a_i+b_i in terms of score difference, then both players want the largest sums. Alice picks largest, Bob next, etc. This is like both players value each stone equally (a_i+b_i), so greedy by sum is optimal. Yes.

But let's double-check with a potential counterexample: a=[8,7,1], b=[0,0,100]? Sums: 8,7,101. Greedy: Alice picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=8,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=7,b=0). Alice=8, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice instead picks stone1 (a=8), Bob picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=7). Alice=15, Bob=100, Bob wins. Greedy good. Another: a=[5,4,100], b=[100,4,0], sums 105,8,100. Greedy Alice picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Bob picks stone3 (a=100,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=4,b=4). Alice=9, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice picks stone3 (a=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=4). Alice=104, Bob=100, Alice wins too. Greedy okay. Need no counterexample.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. If in some strategy Alice gets j and Bob gets i, swapping them (Alice gets i, Bob gets j) changes Alice score by a_i - a_j and Bob score by b_j - b_i? Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

Need maybe if "faulty python code" has "one or more bugs" maybe there is a bug in "combines.sort(reverse=True)" because if we sort by a+b descending, but if Alice and Bob both optimal, the greedy choice is not simply sort by sum? Let's verify with a known counterexample? Wait, is Stone Game VI greedy by sum always optimal? Let's recall LeetCode 1686. The solution is indeed sort by `a[i]+b[i]` descending, then alternate. But let's prove because there is a subtlety: Each stone has value to Alice and Bob. If Alice takes stone i, her net advantage over Bob is a_i + b_i? Let's formalize: Suppose current player chooses stone i. The difference in final scores (current player's score - other player's score) changes by a_i + b_i relative to if other player chose it? Actually if Alice takes i, she gains a_i and Bob doesn't gain b_i, so swing = a_i + b_i. If Bob takes i, swing in Bob's favor = a_i + b_i. Thus each stone contributes its sum to whoever takes it in terms of score difference. Since the total number of stones each player takes is fixed (Alice gets ceil(n/2), Bob floor(n/2)), the optimal is to assign stones with largest sums to Alice? Wait, if each stone's "value" to the taker is a_i+b_i in terms of score difference, then both players want the largest sums. Alice picks largest, Bob next, etc. This is like both players value each stone equally (a_i+b_i), so greedy by sum is optimal. Yes.

But let's double-check with a potential counterexample: a=[8,7,1], b=[0,0,100]? Sums: 8,7,101. Greedy: Alice picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=8,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=7,b=0). Alice=8, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice instead picks stone1 (a=8), Bob picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=7). Alice=15, Bob=100, Bob wins. Greedy good. Another: a=[5,4,100], b=[100,4,0], sums 105,8,100. Greedy Alice picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Bob picks stone3 (a=100,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=4,b=4). Alice=9, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice picks stone3 (a=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=4). Alice=104, Bob=100, Alice wins too. Greedy okay. Need no counterexample.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. If in some strategy Alice gets j and Bob gets i, swapping them (Alice gets i, Bob gets j) changes Alice score by a_i - a_j and Bob score by b_j - b_i? Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

Need maybe if "faulty python code" has "one or more bugs" maybe there is a bug in "combines.sort(reverse=True)" because if we sort by a+b descending, but if Alice and Bob both optimal, the greedy choice is not simply sort by sum? Let's verify with a known counterexample? Wait, is Stone Game VI greedy by sum always optimal? Let's recall LeetCode 1686. The solution is indeed sort by `a[i]+b[i]` descending, then alternate. But let's prove because there is a subtlety: Each stone has value to Alice and Bob. If Alice takes stone i, her net advantage over Bob is a_i + b_i? Let's formalize: Suppose current player chooses stone i. The difference in final scores (current player's score - other player's score) changes by a_i + b_i relative to if other player chose it? Actually if Alice takes i, she gains a_i and Bob doesn't gain b_i, so swing = a_i + b_i. If Bob takes i, swing in Bob's favor = a_i + b_i. Thus each stone contributes its sum to whoever takes it in terms of score difference. Since the total number of stones each player takes is fixed (Alice gets ceil(n/2), Bob floor(n/2)), the optimal is to assign stones with largest sums to Alice? Wait, if each stone's "value" to the taker is a_i+b_i in terms of score difference, then both players want the largest sums. Alice picks largest, Bob next, etc. This is like both players value each stone equally (a_i+b_i), so greedy by sum is optimal. Yes.

But let's double-check with a potential counterexample: a=[8,7,1], b=[0,0,100]? Sums: 8,7,101. Greedy: Alice picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=8,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=7,b=0). Alice=8, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice instead picks stone1 (a=8), Bob picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=7). Alice=15, Bob=100, Bob wins. Greedy good. Another: a=[5,4,100], b=[100,4,0], sums 105,8,100. Greedy Alice picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Bob picks stone3 (a=100,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=4,b=4). Alice=9, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice picks stone3 (a=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=4). Alice=104, Bob=100, Alice wins too. Greedy okay. Need no counterexample.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. If in some strategy Alice gets j and Bob gets i, swapping them (Alice gets i, Bob gets j) changes Alice score by a_i - a_j and Bob score by b_j - b_i? Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

Need maybe if "faulty python code" has "one or more bugs" maybe there is a bug in "combines.sort(reverse=True)" because if we sort by a+b descending, but if Alice and Bob both optimal, the greedy choice is not simply sort by sum? Let's verify with a known counterexample? Wait, is Stone Game VI greedy by sum always optimal? Let's recall LeetCode 1686. The solution is indeed sort by `a[i]+b[i]` descending, then alternate. But let's prove because there is a subtlety: Each stone has value to Alice and Bob. If Alice takes stone i, her net advantage over Bob is a_i + b_i? Let's formalize: Suppose current player chooses stone i. The difference in final scores (current player's score - other player's score) changes by a_i + b_i relative to if other player chose it? Actually if Alice takes i, she gains a_i and Bob doesn't gain b_i, so swing = a_i + b_i. If Bob takes i, swing in Bob's favor = a_i + b_i. Thus each stone contributes its sum to whoever takes it in terms of score difference. Since the total number of stones each player takes is fixed (Alice gets ceil(n/2), Bob floor(n/2)), the optimal is to assign stones with largest sums to Alice? Wait, if each stone's "value" to the taker is a_i+b_i in terms of score difference, then both players want the largest sums. Alice picks largest, Bob next, etc. This is like both players value each stone equally (a_i+b_i), so greedy by sum is optimal. Yes.

But let's double-check with a potential counterexample: a=[8,7,1], b=[0,0,100]? Sums: 8,7,101. Greedy: Alice picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=8,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=7,b=0). Alice=8, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice instead picks stone1 (a=8), Bob picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=7). Alice=15, Bob=100, Bob wins. Greedy good. Another: a=[5,4,100], b=[100,4,0], sums 105,8,100. Greedy Alice picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Bob picks stone3 (a=100,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=4,b=4). Alice=9, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice picks stone3 (a=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=4). Alice=104, Bob=100, Alice wins too. Greedy okay. Need no counterexample.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. If in some strategy Alice gets j and Bob gets i, swapping them (Alice gets i, Bob gets j) changes Alice score by a_i - a_j and Bob score by b_j - b_i? Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Alice picks stone3 (a=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=4). Alice=104, Bob=100, Alice wins too. Greedy okay. Need no counterexample.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. If in some strategy Alice gets j and Bob gets i, swapping them (Alice gets i, Bob gets j) changes Alice score by a_i - a_j and Bob score by b_j - b_i? Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

Actually if Alice takes i, she gains a_i and Bob doesn't gain b_i, so swing = a_i + b_i. If Bob takes i, swing in Bob's favor = a_i + b_i. Thus each stone contributes its sum to whoever takes it in terms of score difference. Since the total number of stones each player takes is fixed (Alice gets ceil(n/2), Bob floor(n/2)), the optimal is to assign stones with largest sums to Alice? Wait, if each stone's "value" to the taker is a_i+b_i in terms of score difference, then both players want the largest sums. Alice picks largest, Bob next, etc. This is like both players value each stone equally (a_i+b_i), so greedy by sum is optimal. Yes.

But let's double-check with a potential counterexample: a=[8,7,1], b=[0,0,100]? Sums: 8,7,101. Greedy: Alice picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=8,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=7,b=0). Alice=8, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice instead picks stone1 (a=8), Bob picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=7). Alice=15, Bob=100, Bob wins. Greedy good. Another: a=[5,4,100], b=[100,4,0], sums 105,8,100. Greedy Alice picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Bob picks stone3 (a=100,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=4,b=4). Alice=9, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice picks stone3 (a=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=4). Alice=104, Bob=100, Alice wins too. Greedy okay. Need no counterexample.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. If in some strategy Alice gets j and Bob gets i, swapping them (Alice gets i, Bob gets j) changes Alice score by a_i - a_j and Bob score by b_j - b_i? Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

Need maybe if "faulty python code" has "one or more bugs" maybe there is a bug in "combines.sort(reverse=True)" because if we sort by a+b descending, but if Alice and Bob both optimal, the greedy choice is not simply sort by sum? Let's verify with a known counterexample? Wait, is Stone Game VI greedy by sum always optimal? Let's recall LeetCode 1686. The solution is indeed sort by `a[i]+b[i]` descending, then alternate. But let's prove because there is a subtlety: Each stone has value to Alice and Bob. If Alice takes stone i, her net advantage over Bob is a_i + b_i? Let's formalize: Suppose current player chooses stone i. The difference in final scores (current player's score - other player's score) changes by a_i + b_i relative to if other player chose it? Actually if Alice takes i, she gains a_i and Bob doesn't gain b_i, so swing = a_i + b_i. If Bob takes i, swing in Bob's favor = a_i + b_i. Thus each stone contributes its sum to whoever takes it in terms of score difference. Since the total number of stones each player takes is fixed (Alice gets ceil(n/2), Bob floor(n/2)), the optimal is to assign stones with largest sums to Alice? Wait, if each stone's "value" to the taker is a_i+b_i in terms of score difference, then both players want the largest sums. Alice picks largest, Bob next, etc. This is like both players value each stone equally (a_i+b_i), so greedy by sum is optimal. Yes.

But let's double-check with a potential counterexample: a=[8,7,1], b=[0,0,100]? Sums: 8,7,101. Greedy: Alice picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=8,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=7,b=0). Alice=8, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice instead picks stone1 (a=8), Bob picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=7). Alice=15, Bob=100, Bob wins. Greedy good. Another: a=[5,4,100], b=[100,4,0], sums 105,8,100. Greedy Alice picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Bob picks stone3 (a=100,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=4,b=4). Alice=9, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice picks stone3 (a=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=4). Alice=104, Bob=100, Alice wins too. Greedy okay. Need no counterexample.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. If in some strategy Alice gets j and Bob gets i, swapping them (Alice gets i, Bob gets j) changes Alice score by a_i - a_j and Bob score by b_j - b_i? Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

Need maybe if "faulty python code" has "one or more bugs" maybe there is a bug in "combines.sort(reverse=True)" because if we sort by a+b descending, but if Alice and Bob both optimal, the greedy choice is not simply sort by sum? Let's verify with a known counterexample? Wait, is Stone Game VI greedy by sum always optimal? Let's recall LeetCode 1686. The solution is indeed sort by `a[i]+b[i]` descending, then alternate. But let's prove because there is a subtlety: Each stone has value to Alice and Bob. If Alice takes stone i, her net advantage over Bob is a_i + b_i? Let's formalize: Suppose current player chooses stone i. The difference in final scores (current player's score - other player's score) changes by a_i + b_i relative to if other player chose it? Actually if Alice takes i, she gains a_i and Bob doesn't gain b_i, so swing = a_i + b_i. If Bob takes i, swing in Bob's favor = a_i + b_i. Thus each stone contributes its sum to whoever takes it in terms of score difference. Since the total number of stones each player takes is fixed (Alice gets ceil(n/2), Bob floor(n/2)), the optimal is to assign stones with largest sums to Alice? Wait, if each stone's "value" to the taker is a_i+b_i in terms of score difference, then both players want the largest sums. Alice picks largest, Bob next, etc. This is like both players value each stone equally (a_i+b_i), so greedy by sum is optimal. Yes.

But let's double-check with a potential counterexample: a=[8,7,1], b=[0,0,100]? Sums: 8,7,101. Greedy: Alice picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=8,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=7,b=0). Alice=8, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice instead picks stone1 (a=8), Bob picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=7). Alice=15, Bob=100, Bob wins. Greedy good. Another: a=[5,4,100], b=[100,4,0], sums 105,8,100. Greedy Alice picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Bob picks stone3 (a=100,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=4,b=4). Alice=9, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice picks stone3 (a=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=4). Alice=104, Bob=100, Alice wins too. Greedy okay. Need no counterexample.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. If in some strategy Alice gets j and Bob gets i, swapping them (Alice gets i, Bob gets j) changes Alice score by a_i - a_j and Bob score by b_j - b_i? Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

Need maybe if "faulty python code" has "one or more bugs" maybe there is a bug in "combines.sort(reverse=True)" because if we sort by a+b descending, but if Alice and Bob both optimal, the greedy choice is not simply sort by sum? Let's verify with a known counterexample? Wait, is Stone Game VI greedy by sum always optimal? Let's recall LeetCode 1686. The solution is indeed sort by `a[i]+b[i]` descending, then alternate. But let's prove because there is a subtlety: Each stone has value to Alice and Bob. If Alice takes stone i, her net advantage over Bob is a_i + b_i? Let's formalize: Suppose current player chooses stone i. The difference in final scores (current player's score - other player's score) changes by a_i + b_i relative to if other player chose it? Actually if Alice takes i, she gains a_i and Bob doesn't gain b_i, so swing = a_i + b_i. If Bob takes i, swing in Bob's favor = a_i + b_i. Thus each stone contributes its sum to whoever takes it in terms of score difference. Since the total number of stones each player takes is fixed (Alice gets ceil(n/2), Bob floor(n/2)), the optimal is to assign stones with largest sums to Alice? Wait, if each stone's "value" to the taker is a_i+b_i in terms of score difference, then both players want the largest sums. Alice picks largest, Bob next, etc. This is like both players value each stone equally (a_i+b_i), so greedy by sum is optimal. Yes.

But let's double-check with a potential counterexample: a=[8,7,1], b=[0,0,100]? Sums: 8,7,101. Greedy: Alice picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=8,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=7,b=0). Alice=8, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice instead picks stone1 (a=8), Bob picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=7). Alice=15, Bob=100, Bob wins. Greedy good. Another: a=[5,4,100], b=[100,4,0], sums 105,8,100. Greedy Alice picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Bob picks stone3 (a=100,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=4,b=4). Alice=9, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice picks stone3 (a=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=4). Alice=104, Bob=100, Alice wins too. Greedy okay. Need no counterexample.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. If in some strategy Alice gets j and Bob gets i, swapping them (Alice gets i, Bob gets j) changes Alice score by a_i - a_j and Bob score by b_j - b_i? Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

Alice=8, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice instead picks stone1 (a=8), Bob picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=7). Alice=15, Bob=100, Bob wins. Greedy good. Another: a=[5,4,100], b=[100,4,0], sums 105,8,100. Greedy Alice picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Bob picks stone3 (a=100,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=4,b=4). Alice=9, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice picks stone3 (a=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=4). Alice=104, Bob=100, Alice wins too. Greedy okay. Need no counterexample.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. If in some strategy Alice gets j and Bob gets i, swapping them (Alice gets i, Bob gets j) changes Alice score by a_i - a_j and Bob score by b_j - b_i? Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

But let's prove because there is a subtlety: Each stone has value to Alice and Bob. If Alice takes stone i, her net advantage over Bob is a_i + b_i? Let's formalize: Suppose current player chooses stone i. The difference in final scores (current player's score - other player's score) changes by a_i + b_i relative to if other player chose it? Actually if Alice takes i, she gains a_i and Bob doesn't gain b_i, so swing = a_i + b_i. If Bob takes i, swing in Bob's favor = a_i + b_i. Thus each stone contributes its sum to whoever takes it in terms of score difference. Since the total number of stones each player takes is fixed (Alice gets ceil(n/2), Bob floor(n/2)), the optimal is to assign stones with largest sums to Alice? Wait, if each stone's "value" to the taker is a_i+b_i in terms of score difference, then both players want the largest sums. Alice picks largest, Bob next, etc. This is like both players value each stone equally (a_i+b_i), so greedy by sum is optimal. Yes.

But let's double-check with a potential counterexample: a=[8,7,1], b=[0,0,100]? Sums: 8,7,101. Greedy: Alice picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=8,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=7,b=0). Alice=8, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice instead picks stone1 (a=8), Bob picks stone3 (a=1,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=7). Alice=15, Bob=100, Bob wins. Greedy good. Another: a=[5,4,100], b=[100,4,0], sums 105,8,100. Greedy Alice picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Bob picks stone3 (a=100,b=0), Alice picks stone2 (a=4,b=4). Alice=9, Bob=0, Alice wins. If Alice picks stone3 (a=100), Bob picks stone1 (a=5,b=100), Alice picks stone2 (a=4). Alice=104, Bob=100, Alice wins too. Greedy okay. Need no counterexample.

Let's maybe think if "sort by a+b descending" is indeed optimal for both players? This is a known "optimal strategy" but let's prove exchange: Consider any two stones i,j with sum_i >= sum_j. If in some strategy Alice gets j and Bob gets i, swapping them (Alice gets i, Bob gets j) changes Alice score by a_i - a_j and Bob score by b_j - b_i? Wait if Alice gets j, Bob gets i: Alice score contribution a_j, Bob b_i. If swap: Alice a_i, Bob b_j. Difference in Alice-Bob = (a_i - b_j) - (a_j - b_i) = (a_i+b_i) - (a_j+b_j) >=0. So swapping improves Alice's margin. Thus optimal for Alice to take higher sum. But Bob also chooses; if Bob gets i and Alice j, Bob's margin is b_i - a_j. If Bob instead gets j and Alice i, Bob's margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (Bob margin after swap?) Let's compute: Bob wants maximize his margin. If Bob gets i and Alice j: Bob margin = b_i - a_j. If Bob gets j and Alice i: Bob margin = b_j - a_i. Difference (j for Bob vs i for Bob) = (b_j - a_i) - (b_i - a_j) = (a_j+b_j) - (a_i+b_i) <=0 if sum_i >= sum_j. So Bob prefers higher sum too. Thus both prefer higher sum, so greedy by sum is optimal. Good.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def stoneGameVI(self, a: List[int], b: List[int]) -> int:
        combines = [(a[i] + b[i], a[i], b[i]) for i in range(len(a))]
        combines.sort(reverse=True)

        alicePoints = 0
        bobPoints = sum(b)

        for i in range(0, len(a), 2):
            alicePoints += combines[i][1]
            bobPoints -= combines[i][2]

        if alicePoints > bobPoints:
            return 1
        elif alicePoints < bobPoints:
            return -1
        return 0
```

=== FIXED CODE ===
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def stoneGameVI(self, a: List[int], b: List[int]) -> int:
        combines = [(a[i] + b[i], a[i], b[i]) for i in range(len(a))]
        combines.sort(reverse=True)

        alicePoints = 0
        bobPoints = sum(b)

        for i in range(0, len(a), 2):
            alicePoints += combines[i][1]
            bobPoints -= combines[i][2]

        if alicePoints > bobPoints:
            return 1
        elif alicePoints < bobPoints:
            return -1
        return 0
